Anomalous dissipation and Euler flows Thanks: LSz gratefully acknowledges the support of the Deutsche Forschungsgemeinschaft (DFG, German Research Foundation) through GZ SZ 325/2-1. Thanks: The authors would like to thank Theodore Drivas and Massimo Sorella for their helpful remarks on an earlier version of this paper.
Jan Burczak, László Székelyhidi, Jr, Bian Wu

Abstract
. We show anomalous dissipation of scalars advected by weak solutions to the incompressible Euler equations with C ( 1 / 3 ) − C^{(\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}})^{-}} regularity, for an arbitrary initial datum in H ˙ 1 ​ ( 𝕋 3 ) \dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{3}) . This is the first rigorous derivation of zeroth law of scalar turbulence, where the scalar is advected by solution to an equation of hydrodynamics (unforced and deterministic). As a byproduct of our method, we provide a typicality statement for the drift, and recover certain desired properties of turbulence, including a lower bound on scalar variance commensurate with the Richardson pair dispersion hypothesis.



fourierlargesymbols147

1.
Introduction

We consider the Cauchy problem for the linear advection-diffusion equation

\begin{split}\partial_{t}\rho_{\kappa}+u\cdot\nabla\rho_{\kappa}-\kappa\Delta\rho_{\kappa}&=0,\\
\rho_{\kappa}|_{t=0}&=\rho_{in},\end{split}

(1.1)

on the
3
-dimensional flat torus
\mathbb{T}^{3}
. The parameter
\kappa>0
represents molecular diffusivity, and the quantity
\rho_{\kappa}:\mathbb{T}^{3}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
is a passive scalar which is advected by a given divergence-free vector field
u:\mathbb{T}^{3}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
for some time
T>0
. The energy equality reads

\frac{1}{2}\|\rho_{\kappa}(T)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\|\rho_{in}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}-\kappa\int_{0}^{T}\|\nabla\rho_{\kappa}(t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\,dt.

Even though the drift
u
does not feature in this identity, it is well known that the nature of
u
crucially affects the decay rate of
\frac{1}{2}\|\rho_{\kappa}(T)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}
. Indeed, one typically expects advection by the flow to generate small scales which are then much more efficiently damped by the diffusion. In the context of scalar turbulence, where
u
is the velocity field of a turbulent flow, the expectation is that the effect of small-scale generation via advection balances diffusion, so that the rate of dissipation of scalar variance

\kappa\int_{0}^{T}\|\nabla\rho_{\kappa}(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,dt

(1.2)

is bounded from below by a positive constant, independently of
\kappa>0
. This is referred to as
anomalous dissipation
. The purpose of this work is to establish anomalous dissipation for a large class of drifts, which are themselves weak solutions of the incompressible Euler equations

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}u+u\cdot\nabla u+\nabla p=0,\\
\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits u=0.\end{cases}

(1.3)

Our main result, in simplified form, reads as follows.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
0<T_{0}<\infty
and
0<\beta<1/3
. There exists a weak solution
u\in C^{\beta}(\mathbb{T}^{3}\times[0,T_{0}])
to (
1.3
) such that, for every non-zero initial datum
\rho_{in}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{3})
with zero mean, the family of unique solutions
\{\rho_{\kappa}\}_{\kappa>0}
to (
1.1
) satisfies for any
T\leq T_{0}

\limsup_{\kappa\to 0}\;\kappa\int_{0}^{T}\|\nabla\rho_{\kappa}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,dt\geq c_{0}\|\rho_{in}\|_{L^{2}}^{2},

(1.4)

where
c_{0}>0
depends only on
\beta
,
\frac{\|\rho_{in}\|_{L^{2}}}{\|\nabla\rho_{in}\|_{L^{2}}}
and
T
.

In order to prove this result, we develop an approach that is able to simultaneously handle two important but so far unrelated recent developments in the field: the construction of weak solutions of the incompressible Euler equations via convex integration, leading to a resolution of the celebrated Onsager’s conjecture
[
DLS13
,
Ise18
,
BLSV19
]
; and the work of S. Armstrong and V. Vicol on implementing iterated homogenization to construct incompressible time-dependent vector fields in 2D, exhibiting anomalous dissipation of scalar variance
[
AV23
]
. Apart from highlighting the strong similarities at the technical level, this combination leads naturally to a more general statement than Theorem
1.1
, which highlights two key features of the construction. The general statement is deferred to Section
3
(see Theorem
3.1
), but for the purposes of this introduction let us briefly comment on the main features.

1.1.
h-principle

First of all, our approach yields not just a single vector field, but a large class of such fields. Indeed, it is well known that there is a close analogy between weak solutions constructed via convex integration and Gromov’s h-principle, a principle referring to a high level of non-uniqueness and flexibility. We refer the interested reader to one of the many surveys on the subject
[
DLS12
,
Szé14
,
DLS17
,
BV19
]
. In particular, within the context of weak solutions of the Euler equations, one obtains certain density statements. In the setting of this paper this means:

Strong density:

For any smooth Euler solution
\bar{u}
there is sequence
\{u^{(n)}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of Euler solutions with properties as in Theorem
1.1
, such that
\|u^{(n)}-\bar{u}\|_{C^{0}(\mathbb{T}^{3}\times[0,T])}\to 0
as
n\to 0
.

Weak density:

For any smooth solenoidal
\bar{u}
there is sequence
\{u^{(n)}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of Euler solutions with properties as in Theorem
1.1
, such that
\|u^{(n)}-\bar{u}\|_{C([0,T];H^{-1}(\mathbb{T}^{3}))}\to 0
as
n\to 0
.

The constant
c_{0}
in Theorem
1.1
depends in both of cases above additionally on
n
.

We remark that obtaining analogous statements within other constructions of vector fields inducing anomalous dissipation (see Section
1.4
) seems challenging.

1.2.
Continuous-in-time dissipation and Richardson hypothesis

Secondly, as in the work of Armstrong-Vicol
[
AV23
]
, in our construction anomalous dissipation occurs continuously in time, in agreement with the postulate of statistical stationarity in homogeneous isotropic turbulence. In fact, the constant
c_{0}
of (
1.4
) is computed explicitly in Theorem
3.1
in terms of the data. In particular, the lower bound (
3.4
) in Theorem
3.1
implies the following: for any
0<\beta<1/3
there is
\varepsilon_{\beta}
such that for any
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{\beta}
the statement of Theorem
1.1
holds with constant
c_{0}
satisfying

c_{0}\geq c_{\varepsilon}\min\big\{\ell_{0}^{-2p_{\varepsilon}}\ell_{in}^{2p_{\varepsilon}-2}T^{p_{\varepsilon}},\ell_{in}^{2\varepsilon}\big\},

(1.5)

where
p_{\varepsilon}=\frac{2+\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon\frac{2+\varepsilon}{1+\varepsilon}-(1+\varepsilon)\beta}
,
\ell_{0}:=\sup_{f\in\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{3})}\frac{\|f\|_{L^{2}}}{\|\nabla f\|_{L^{2}}}
, and

\ell_{in}:=\frac{\|\rho_{in}\|_{L^{2}}}{\|\nabla\rho_{in}\|_{L^{2}}}
.
Observe that
p_{\varepsilon}\to\frac{2}{1-\beta}
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, leading to the asymptotic rate
T^{\frac{2}{1-\beta}}
in (
1.5
). This is in agreement with the Richardson hypothesis on pair dispersion in turbulence
1
1

1

We are grateful to Theodore Drivas for pointing out this connection to us.
. In modern language this hypothesis reads as follows. Denote by
(X^{\kappa}_{t})_{t}
the stochastic trajectories of the advection-diffusion (
1.1
). Then
\rho_{\kappa}(t)=\mathbb{E}(\rho_{in}\circ X^{\kappa}_{t})
, and one can write
\|\rho_{in}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=\mathbb{E}\|\rho_{in}\circ X^{\kappa}_{t}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}
, thus

\begin{split}\kappa\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla\rho_{\kappa}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,ds&=\frac{1}{2}\left(\|\rho_{in}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}-\|\rho_{\kappa}(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}{\rm{Var}}(\rho_{in}\circ X^{\kappa}_{t}).\end{split}

(1.6)

In particular, when
\rho_{in}
has uniform random space gradient with isotropic statistics
G\,\mathrm{Id}
then, see
[
DE17
]
formula (2.17),

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}{\rm{Var}}(\rho_{in}\circ X^{\kappa}_{t})=\frac{1}{4}G^{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\mathbb{E}^{(1),(2)}|X^{\kappa,(1)}_{t}-X^{\kappa,(2)}_{t}|^{2}=:\delta_{t}^{2},

where superscripts
{(1),(2)}
indicate two independent Brownian ensembles. Thus

\begin{split}\dot{\delta}_{t}&\lesssim\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\mathbb{E}^{(1),(2)}|\dot{X}^{\kappa,(1)}_{t}-\dot{X}^{\kappa,(2)}_{t}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}=\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\mathbb{E}^{(1),(2)}|u\circ X^{\kappa,(1)}_{t}-u\circ X^{\kappa,(2)}_{t}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&\sim\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\mathbb{E}^{(1),(2)}|X^{\kappa,(1)}_{t}-X^{\kappa,(2)}_{t}|^{2\beta}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim\delta^{\beta}_{t},\end{split}

where the second line uses
C^{\beta}
regularity of the drift. Assuming that in the above the inequalities
\lesssim
can be replaced by
\sim
, we obtain the ODE
\dot{\delta}_{t}\sim\delta^{\beta}_{t}
, and deduce
\delta^{2}_{t}\sim T^{\frac{2}{1-\beta}}
.
Observe that, in particular, the limit in the allowed regularity of the drift:
T^{\frac{2}{1-\beta}}\overset{\beta\to\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}{\to}T^{3}
recovers the classical Richardson (or Richardson-Obukhov) scaling.

Let us now discuss in more detail the physical and mathematical context of our work.

1.3.
Turbulence

In the 1949 paper
[
Obu49
]
titled ’Structure of the Temperature Field in Turbulent Flow’ A. N. Obukhov writes

In other words, the turbulent motion inside a thermally heterogeneous medium with gradients which are initially weak can contribute to the local gradients of temperature, which are subsequently smoothed out by the action of molecular heat conductivity.

This statement is precisely a prediction that dissipation caused by little (’molecular’)
\kappa
present in (
1.1
) can be amplified through advection
u\cdot\nabla
by a turbulent velocity field
u
.
The responsible mechanism should be transfer of modes towards higher frequencies/smaller scales, where eventually diffusivity at the ‘molecular’ length-scale steps in. This prediction of Obukhov has been further corroborated by phenomenological, experimental and numerical arguments, see the classical Yaglom
[
Yag49
]
, Corrsin
[
Cor51
]
, Batchelor
[
Bat59
]
,
and more recent
[
SS10
]
,
[
DSY05
]
,
[
SS00
]
.
Furthermore, the expected amplification of dissipation is supposed to be sufficient to keep the quantity (
1.2
) bounded away from zero, thus causing “anomalous dissipation”.
Thus, it is strongly believed that scalar anomalous dissipation is a key feature and manifestation of turbulence.

On the other hand, it is argued that turbulent flows are irregular. More precisely, L. Onsager conjectured in his famous 1949 note on statistical hydrodynamics
[
Ons49
]
that the threshold regularity for the validity of energy conservation in the class of Hölder continuous weak solutions of the Euler equations is the exponent
1/3
. Onsager’s interest in this issue came from an effort to explain the primary mechanism of energy dissipation in turbulence, one that persists even in the absence of viscosity. Thus, the implicit suggestion was that Hölder continuous weak solutions of the Euler equations may be an appropriate mathematical description of turbulent flows in the inviscid limit, corresponding to a form of
ideal turbulence
.

We recall that the central analytical model of passive scalar turbulence, the Kraichnan 1968 model
[
Kra68
]
, is based on Gaussian random velocity fields consisting of spatially non-smooth velocities with a K41-type scaling of increments and white-noise correlation in time. This model is able to recover various scaling laws, including the Obukhov-Corrsin
k^{-5/3}
spectrum in the inertial-convective regime (Schmidt number
=O(1)
) and the Batchelor
k^{-1}
spectrum in the viscous-convective regime (Schmidt number
\gg 1
). In particular, Kraichnan’s model is known to exhibit anomalous dissipation; for more details we refer to
[
BGK98
,
FGV01
,
Sre19
,
DE17
,
Row23
]
. Lifting these results to a rigorous PDE setting has remained a central challenge in the field.

In a recent breakthrough work
[
BBPS22a
,
BBPS22b
]
J. Bedrossian, A. Blumenthal and S. Punshon-Smith were able to show almost sure exponential mixing of Lagrangian trajectories and consequently verify Batchelor’s law on the cumulative power spectrum for velocity fields given by solutions of randomly forced 2D Navier-Stokes and hyperviscous 3D Navier-Stokes at finite Reynolds number. However, in these examples realizations of the velocity field are almost surely uniformly bounded in
C^{1}
so that anomalous dissipation in the sense of (
1.4
) is not possible (see below). In contrast, our focus in this work is on the complementary Obukhov-Corrsin range, where, in analogy with Onsager’s conjecture and its relation to Kolmogorov’s K41 theory, we study Obukhov’s prediction of anomalous dissipation in the context of ideal turbulence.

1.4.
Anomalous dissipation in PDEs

For any fixed initial datum, the unique solution
\rho_{\kappa}
of (
1.1
) converges weakly along subsequences
\kappa_{j}\to 0
to a distributional solution of the transport equation
\partial_{t}\rho+u\cdot\nabla\rho=0
. If the advecting velocity field
u
is sufficiently regular (classically
u\in L^{1}(W^{1,\infty})
, but
u\in L^{1}(W^{1,1})
or even
u\in L^{1}(BV)
suffices, see, respectively,
[
DL89
]
and
[
Amb04
]
), then the transport equation admits a uniquely defined measure-preserving Lagrangian flow map, which can be used to define a unique renormalized solution. In turn, the renormalized solution will be the unique inviscid limit
[
BCC22a
]
- however, we mention that other, non-renormalized solutions of the transport equation may nevertheless exist
[
MS18
,
MS19
]
. Thus, for sufficiently regular velocity fields anomalous dissipation is excluded, since measure preservation implies conservation of the
L^{2}
norm.

On the other hand, for less regular velocity fields
u
anomalous dissipation as well as non-uniqueness in the inviscid limit may occur, as has been shown in a number of examples, beginning with the work of T. Drivas, T. Elgindi, G. Iyer and I.-J. Jeong
[
DEIJ22
]
. In
[
DEIJ22
]
the authors constructed, for any initial datum
\rho_{in}\in H^{2}
and any
\alpha<1
, a divergence-free velocity field
u\in L^{1}([0,T_{0}];C^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))
for which one has anomalous dissipation in the sense that (
1.4
) holds for
T=T_{0}
. The construction of the advecting velocity
u
is based on alternating shear flows with a carefully chosen sequence of length- and time-scales concentrating at the critical final time
t=T
. In particular, there is no anomalous dissipation for any time
T<T_{0}
. This construction was later substantially improved in
[
CCS23
]
, on the one hand by complementing with uniform estimates on the solution of the type
\rho\in L^{\infty}([0,T_{0}];C^{\beta}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))
with
\alpha+2\beta<1
(in agreement with the power-law prediction of Obukhov-Corrsin) and on the other hand by showing non-uniqueness of the inviscid limit. Recently in
[
EL23
]
, Elgindi and Liss have constructed a divergence-free vector field which satisfies
u\in C^{\infty}([0,T];C^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))
for every
\alpha<1
and which gives rise to anomalous dissipation in equation (
1.1
) for every smooth initial datum; in fact
u
is merely logarithmically below Lipschitz regularity and the initial datum is only required to be in
\rho_{in}\in H^{(\nicefrac{{7}}{{5}})^{+}}\cap W^{1,\infty}
. All these constructions have a common feature: the velocity fields are based on alternating shear flows, where one has very good control over mixing properties of the Lagrangian flow-map (inspired by
[
Dep03
,
Pie94
,
Aiz78
]
), and the dissipation anomaly takes place at a single “critical” time
t=T_{0}
.

Further, in the recent papers
[
BDL23
]
,
[
BCC+22b
]
, partially drawing from
[
JY22a
,
JY22b
]
, it was shown that the alternating shear-flow constructions above can also be seen as velocity fields which are solutions of the
forced
Navier-Stokes equations exhibiting anomalous dissipation of kinetic energy, thereby giving rigorous verification of the ‘zeroth law of turbulence’, at the cost of introducing a forcing term. In a different direction, in
[
HPZZ23
]
the authors consider the Navier-Stokes equations with large stochastic forcing and friction, and showed for such velocity fields
total
dissipation at the critical time, i.e.
\lim_{t\to T_{0}}\|\rho_{\kappa}(t)\|_{L^{2}}=0
for any
\kappa>0
, by letting the strength of the forcing and of the friction blow up as
t\to T_{0}
. Finally, we mention the recent interesting work
[
HT23
]
, where the authors construct a velocity field which, although does not exhibit anomalous dissipation in the sense of (
1.4
), leads to a (non-unique) solution in the inviscid limit
\rho_{\kappa}\rightharpoonup\rho
as
\kappa\to 0
with energy that jumps down and then up again.

1.4.1.
Homogenization, the Armstrong-Vicol approach, and our result

The classical concept of eddy diffusivity is a prediction that molecular dissipation is amplified by presence of small-scale fluid eddies (see U. Frisch
[
Fri95
]
, Section 9.6). This concept assumes the existence of two well-separated scales, the macroscopic integral scale and the microscopic scale of eddies, and has been successfully formalised within the classical homogenization theory, see

[
PP81
,
MPP85
,
Fri89
,
FP94
,
MK99
]
. However, fully developed turbulence has neither merely two scales, nor are the scales well-separated. Even though the homogenization methods has been developed to iterated quantitative homogenization, which includes multiple scales and non-infinite separation (see e.g.
[
NSX20
]
), the lack of any apparent separation of scales in turbulence has been perceived as a major obstacle to applying homogenization to modern turbulence theory (see e.g. the discussion in Section 3 of
[
MK99
]
). Despite these reservations, S. Armstrong and V. Vicol
[
AV23
]
have recently successfully combined homogenization methods with an infinite iterative scheme to construct a divergence-free vector field with infinitely many active scales, which induces anomalous dissipation. This crucial development has substantially inspired our work. On a heuristic level, the lack of scale separation in fully developed turbulent flow is overcome by the choice of slightly super-exponential growth of active wavenumbers/scales, which does lead to an increasing separation of scales along the iteration - a technical trick which to our knowledge has been introduced into the field in the paper
[
DLS13
]
and has been used since ubiquitously.

The methodology of
[
AV23
]
has several advantages over the previously mentioned ways of constructing velocities that give rise to anomalous diffusion:
First of all, it is not based on the analysis of the inviscid limit and on mixing properties of the Lagrangian flow map, consequently it becomes compatible with the existence of an inertial range in turbulence. Secondly, in works based on alternating shear flows focussing towards a singular time, the anomalous dissipation only occurs at only one instant of time, a major drawback in the context of turbulent flows in light of the statistical time-invariance, whereas in
[
AV23
]
the dissipation anomaly has fractal structure in time. Thirdly, as in classical homogenization, the method of
[
AV23
]
yields anomalous dissipation for arbitrary initial data, in contrast with previous constructions based on convex integration
[
MS18
,
MS19
,
BCDL21
,
MS20
]
, where the density is constructed in parallel to the velocity field scale-by-scale.

It is worth mentioning that, although the framework of iterative homogenization comes with several advantages mentioned above, it has one major drawback in comparison with the tools developed based on mixing and alternating shear flows summarized in Section
1.4
above: the technique seems to be restricted to velocity fields with spatial regularity
C^{(\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}})^{-}}
- although this seems natural in light of Onsager’s conjecture and weak solutions of (
1.3
) and the scaling analysis of effective diffusivities (both in
[
AV23
]
and in our work) gives compelling evidence that the bound
\beta<1/3
in Theorem
1.1
is not merely technical and appears even if
u
is not required to solve any PDE.

The main step forward that we make compared with
[
AV23
]
is that our velocity field solves incompressible Euler equation. To our knowledge this is the first result on anomalous dissipation, where the advecting vector field is itself the solution of an unforced, deterministic PDE. Consequently we provide the first deterministic link on the PDE level between the Obukhov-Corrsin theory for scalar transport and the Onsager theory of ideal turbulence. On the methodological level, we interpret iterative quantitative homogenization in parallel with convex integration, with the basic principle in mind that whilst convex integration is a form of “inverse renormalization” strategy, iterative quantitative homogenization can be seen as “forward renormalization”. On a more technical level we need to overcome a number of challenges:

•

First of all, a key issue in applying homogenization techniques is to obtain good control of the corrector. In our setting, which is not based on shear flows but on Mikado flows, obtaining the required control from elliptic estimates seems challenging (indeed, Appendix A in
[
AV23
]
points to possible obstruction). Instead, we modify the construction of Mikado flows in such a way that allows us to
explicitly
construct the corrector with a Mikado-like structure.

•

The construction of weak solutions to the Euler equations in
[
BLSV19
]
relies on the gluing technique introduced by P. Isett in
[
Ise18
]
, which requires introducing an alternating arrangement of time intervals over which the small-scale fluctuations are turned on and off - and both states have to persist over time-scales which are of the order of the eddy turnover time. In order to have sufficient control of the dissipation rate, this requires a careful fine-tuning of the parameters and sharper bounds in the convex integration scheme of
[
BLSV19
]
.

•

Due to the gluing technique in the convex integration construction, the effective diffusivity coefficient obtained from the homogenization step is oscillating in time, and an additional temporal averaging step is needed to obtain a global effective diffusivity. Our approach to deal with this issue is again inspired by
[
AV23
]
, but due to the requirements given by the gluing scheme we need sharper bounds. Indeed, in this paper we have identified and isolated this as an issue of independent interest, and therefore present the temporal averaging in Section
6
as a standalone result that can be applied independently.

1.5.
Related topics: enhanced dissipation, mixing, and beyond

Within or above Lipschitz regularity for
u
, divergence-free drift can of course also assist dissipation, but in a weaker sense than anomalous dissipation. This is usually referred to as enhanced dissipation. The enhanced dissipation by a single shear flow, or another simple “lower dimensional” flow is well studied, with roots in the computations by Kelvin and Kolmogorov
[
Kol34
]
, compare
[
BCZ17
,
CZEW20
,
CCZW21
,
ABN22
,
ZG23
]
and references therein. In such cases one needs to restrict the initial datum to a class not orthogonal to the shearing direction, but the setting usually allows to extract precise decay rates. For a more general related approach see
[
Vuk21
]
. The general (spectral) condition on arbitrary
u
for dissipation enhancement in the Lipschitz case has been provided in the seminal
[
CKRZ08
]
(for an example of a related optimal rate, see
[
ELM23
]
), whose results has been recently strenghtened and reproved by a different method in
[
Wei21
]
.

Let us point out, without getting into details, natural connections between enhanced dissipation and mixing (which is the phenomenon of inviscid-case migration towards smaller scales caused by a vector field), see
[
CZDE20
]
and the recent survey
[
ZCIM23
]
with its references. Mixing phenomena are present also naturally in the kinetic setting (e.g. Landau damping) cf.
[
MV11
]
, and in suppression of chemotactic blowup, including the recent beautiful
[
HKY23
]
.

There are also interesting developments in the stochastic setting. In this context we mention the recent work
[
CMOW23
]
, inspired by
[
BK91
]
,
[
SZ06
]
, where optimal quantitative enhanced dissipation estimates are obtained for a random drift given by the Gaussian free field - the strategy there, based on a renormalization strategy of computing effective diffusivities scale-by-scale, is very much reminiscent of our point of view.

1.6.
Notation

We use mostly standard notation, the less standard is explained where it appears. To follow several vector calculus identitites, one should keep in mind that the vectors are column vectors. We will often write
a\lesssim b
, which means there is an (implicit) constant
C
, such that
a\leq Cb
. Importantly, such implicit constants are uniform over iterative steps we will perform.

1.7.
Paper outline

Section
2
presents fundamental building blocks for our construction. The technical results needed in Section
2
: the homogenization Proposition
5.1
(and its corollaries), the time averaging Proposition
6.1
and the convex integration Proposition
2.1
are proved, respectively, in Section
5
, Section
6
, Section
7
. Section
3
contains the proofs of our main results. Section
4
gathers mostly standard energy estimates needed in the paper. We have tried to present the results of Sections
4
-
7
in a self-contained manner, as we believe they may be of independent interest.

2.
Building blocks for proof of Theorem
1.1

In this section we provide the key ingredients for our proofs, and postpone technical details to subsequent sections. As mentioned in the introduction, our strategy involves a ”backward renormalization” in constructing the velocity field in Section
2.1
(in the sense of an iteration from large-scale to small-scale,
u_{q}\mapsto u_{q+1}\mapsto\dots
, based on convex-integration ideas), and a ”forward renormalization” in constructing the passive tracer in Section
2.2
(in the sense of an iteration from small-scale to large-scale,
\rho_{q+1}\mapsto\rho_{q}\mapsto\dots
, based on homogenization-related arguments). Both of these sections have an inductive flavour. In order to initialise the induction, we will need also an h-principle argument, presented in Section
2.3
.

2.1.
Construction of the vector field - Convex integration

The general scheme for producing Hölder continuous weak solutions of the Euler equations (
1.3
) is by now well understood. Our approach below closely follows the presentation in
[
BLSV19
]
. One proceeds via an inductive process on a sequence of approximate solutions
u_{q}
with associated Reynolds defect
\mathring{R}_{q}
and pressure
p_{q}
, for
q=0,1,2,\dots
, which satisfy the
Euler-Reynolds system

\begin{split}\partial_{t}u_{q}+\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits(u_{q}\otimes u_{q})+\nabla p_{q}&=\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits\mathring{R}_{q}\,,\\
\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits u_{q}&=0\,,\end{split}

(2.1)

with constraints

\mathrm{tr\,}\mathring{R}_{q}(x,t)=0,\quad\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}u_{q}(x,t)\,dx=0,\quad\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}p_{q}(x,t)\,dx=0.

(2.2)

We note in passing that these normalizations determine the pressure
p_{q}
uniquely from
(u_{q},\mathring{R}_{q})
so that one may speak of the pair
(u_{q},\mathring{R}_{q})
being a solution of (
2.1
).

2.1.1.
Inductive assumptions

The induction process involves a set of
inductive estimates
. These estimates are in terms of a frequency parameter
\lambda_{q}
and amplitude
\delta_{q}
, which are given by

\lambda_{q}:=2\pi\lceil a^{(b^{q})}\rceil,\quad\delta_{q}:=\lambda_{q}^{-2\beta}

(2.3)

where
\lceil x\rceil
denotes the smallest integer
n\geq x
,
a\gg 1
is a large parameter,
b>1
is close to
1
and
0<\beta<\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}
is the exponent of Theorem
1.1
. The parameters
a
and
b
are then related to
\beta
.
With these parameters the inductive estimates take the form
2
2

2

In
[
BLSV19
]
an additional estimate is added for
\|u_{q}\|_{C^{0}}
, but it turns out this can be avoided. The only place where a bound on
\|u_{q}\|_{C^{0}}
was needed in
[
BLSV19
]
is in
[
BLSV19
, Proposition 5.9]
, but as we show below, the (much worse) bound induced by (
2.5
) for
n=1
suffices to control the relevant terms - see Proposition
7.3
and Lemma
7.15
.

\displaystyle\left\|\mathring{R}_{q}\right\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\leq\delta_{q+1}\lambda_{q}^{-\gamma_{R}},

(2.4)

\displaystyle\left\|u_{q}\right\|_{C^{n}}

\displaystyle\leq M\delta_{q}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q}^{n}\quad\textrm{ for }n=1,2,\dots,\bar{N},

(2.5)

\displaystyle\left|e(t)-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\left|u_{q}\right|^{2}\,dx-\bar{e}\delta_{q+1}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\delta_{q+1}\lambda_{q}^{-\gamma_{E}},

(2.6)

where
\gamma_{R},\gamma_{E}>0
are small parameters,
\bar{N}\in\mathbb{N}
is a large parameter to be chosen suitably (depending on
\beta>0
and
b>1
), and
\bar{e}>0
,
M\geq 1
are universal constants, which will be fixed throughout the iteration and depend on the particular geometric form of the perturbing building blocks - they will be specified in Definition
7.10
and
7.14
. We remark that in
[
BLSV19
]
only the case
\bar{N}=1
is required in (
2.5
) and (
2.6
) is slightly weaker. Moreover, in
[
BLSV19
]
a generic small parameter
\alpha>0
is used in place of
\gamma_{R},\gamma_{E}
, but for our purposes we need to choose these small corrections more carefully.

For notational convenience we introduce

\mathring{\delta}_{q+1}:=\delta_{q+1}\lambda_{q}^{-\gamma_{R}}=\lambda_{q}^{-2b\beta-\gamma_{R}},

so that (
2.4
) can be written as
\left\|\mathring{R}_{q}\right\|_{C^{0}}\leq\mathring{\delta}_{q+1}
.

2.1.2.
Parameter choices

The inductive construction for passing from
u_{q}
to
u_{q+1}
involves three steps:
mollification
,
gluing
and
perturbation
. In these steps two more scales are introduced, an adjusted length-scale
\ell_{q}
and an adjusted time-scale
\tau_{q}
. In our case these will be defined as

\ell_{q}:=\lambda_{q}^{-1-\gamma_{L}},\quad\tau_{q}:=\lambda_{q}^{-1+\beta-\gamma_{T}},

(2.7)

where
\gamma_{L},\gamma_{T}
are additional small parameters to be chosen suitably in dependence of
\beta,b
. It is worth pointing out that, if one were to set
\gamma_{L}=\gamma_{T}=0
, then
\ell_{q},\tau_{q}
would be the natural (dimensionally consistent) length- and time-scales induced by the velocity field
u_{q}
(cf. (
2.3
) and (
2.5
)). Indeed, we can think of
\delta_{q}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
having physical dimension of velocity, i.e.
LT^{-1}
, and
\lambda_{q}^{-1}
having physical dimension of length
L
.

Moreover, setting
\gamma_{R}=0
would be the consistent estimate in light of the basic principle in convex integration, that the error
R_{q}
is cancelled by the new average stress
\langle(u_{q+1}-u_{q})\otimes(u_{q+1}-u_{q})\rangle
.

In addition to these small parameters we will also use
\alpha>0
, as is also done in
[
BLSV19
]
, to take care of the lack of Schauder estimates in
C^{0},C^{1},\dots
spaces. Thus, in summary, we will use the following set of additional parameters, which will all be chosen depending on
\beta,b
:

•

\gamma_{R}\in(0,1)
: smallness of
\|\mathring{R}_{q}\|_{C^{0}}
with respect to
\|R_{q}\|_{C^{0}}
,

•

\gamma_{L}\in(0,1)
: smallness of
\ell_{q}
with respect to
\lambda_{q}^{-1}
,

•

\gamma_{T}\in(0,1)
: smallness of
\tau_{q}
with respect to
(\delta_{q}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q})^{-1}
,

•

\gamma_{E}\in(0,1)
: smallness of energy gap with respect to
\|R_{q}\|_{C^{0}}
,

•

\alpha\in(0,1)
: Schauder exponent,

•

\bar{N}\in\mathbb{N}
: number of derivatives in the induction.

With a suitable choice of these parameters we have the following analogue of
[
BLSV19
, Proposition 2.1]
:

Proposition 2.1
.

There exist universal constants
M\geq 1
,
\bar{e}>0
with the following property. Assume
0<\beta<\frac{1}{3}
and

1<b<\frac{1-\beta}{2\beta}\,.

(2.8)

Further, assume that
\gamma_{T},\gamma_{R},\gamma_{E}>0
satisfy

\max\{\gamma_{T}+b\gamma_{R},\gamma_{E}\}<(b-1)\bigl(1-(2b+1)\beta\bigr).

(2.9)

For any sufficiently small
\gamma_{L}>0
there exists
\bar{N}\in\mathbb{N}
, depending on
\beta,b,\gamma_{T},\gamma_{R},\gamma_{E}
, such that for any sufficiently small
\alpha>0
and any strictly positive smooth function
e:[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
there exists
a_{0}\gg 1
such that the following holds:

Let
(u_{q},\mathring{R}_{q})
be a smooth solution of (
2.1
) satisfying the estimates (
2.4
)–(
2.6
) with
\lambda_{q},\delta_{q}
given by (
2.3
) for any fixed
a\geq a_{0}
. Then there exists another solution
(u_{q+1},\mathring{R}_{q+1})
to (
2.1
) satisfying (
2.4
)–(
2.6
) with
q
replaced by
q+1
, and we have

\left\|u_{q+1}-u_{q}\right\|_{C^{0}}+\frac{1}{\lambda_{q+1}}\left\|u_{q+1}-u_{q}\right\|_{C^{1}}\leq M\delta_{q+1}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}},

(2.10)

The new velocity field
u_{q+1}
is obtained as

u_{q+1}:=\bar{u}_{q}+w_{q+1},

(2.11)

where
\bar{u}_{q}
is constructed from
u_{q}
via Isett’s gluing procedure
[
Ise18
]
, and
w_{q+1}
is the new perturbation consisting of a deformed family of Mikado flows. In Sections
2.1.3
-
2.1.4
below we now sketch the proof of Proposition
2.1
. The detailed proof, which is very much based on
[
BLSV19
]
, is given in Section
7
.

2.1.3.
Gluing procedure

The gluing procedure amounts to the following statement:

Proposition 2.2
.

Within the setting of Proposition
2.1
we have the following statement. Let
(u_{q},\mathring{R}_{q})
be a smooth solution of (
2.1
) satisfying the estimates (
2.4
)–(
2.6
). Then there exists another solution
(\bar{u}_{q},\mathring{\bar{R}}_{q})
to (
2.1
) such that

\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits\mathring{\bar{R}}_{q}\subset\mathbb{T}^{3}\times\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}(i\tau_{q}+\tfrac{1}{3}\tau_{q},i\tau_{q}+\tfrac{2}{3}\tau_{q})

(2.12)

and the following estimates hold for any
N\geq 0
:

\displaystyle\|\bar{u}_{q}\|_{C^{N+1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\delta_{q}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q}\ell_{q}^{-N}\,,

(2.13)

\displaystyle\|\mathring{\bar{R}}_{q}\|_{C^{N+\alpha}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathring{\delta}_{q+1}\ell_{q}^{-N-2\alpha}\,,

(2.14)

\displaystyle\|(\partial_{t}+\bar{u}_{q}\cdot\nabla)\mathring{\bar{R}}_{q}\|_{C^{N+\alpha}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\tau_{q}^{-1}\mathring{\delta}_{q+1}\ell_{q}^{-N-2\alpha}\,,

(2.15)

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}|\bar{u}_{q}|^{2}-|u_{q}|^{2}\,dx\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathring{\delta}_{q+1}\,.

(2.16)

Moreover, the vector potentials of
u_{q}
and
\bar{u}_{q}
satisfy

\|z_{q}-\bar{z}_{q}\|_{C^{\alpha}}\lesssim\tau_{q}\mathring{\delta}_{q+1}\ell_{q}^{-\alpha}\,.

(2.17)

Here and in the sequel, the vector potential
z
of a divergence-free velocity field
u
is given by the Biot-Savart operator on
\mathbb{T}^{3}
, defined as
\mathcal{B}=(-\Delta)^{-1}\mathop{\rm curl}\nolimits
, so that
z=\mathcal{B}u
is the unique solution of

\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits z=0\qquad\textrm{ and }\qquad\mathop{\rm curl}\nolimits z=u\,,

(2.18)

recalling that we assume zero spatial average of
u
.

Proposition
2.2
is restated below as Corollary
7.7
and is a direct consequence of Corollary
7.4
and Proposition
7.6
.

2.1.4.
The perturbation

The formula for the new perturbation
w_{q+1}
uses Mikado flows, which we recall here briefly.
Mikado flows were introduced originally in
[
DS17
]
and widely used since in applications of convex integration to fluid dynamics.

We fix a finite set
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
consisting of nonzero vectors
\vec{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
with rational coordinates, and for each
\vec{k}\in\Lambda
let
\varphi_{\vec{k}}
be a periodic function with the properties

•

\vec{k}\cdot\nabla\varphi_{\vec{k}}=0
,

•

For any
\vec{k}\neq\vec{k}^{\prime}\in\Lambda
we have
\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits\varphi_{\vec{k}}\cap\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits\varphi_{\vec{k}^{\prime}}=\emptyset
,

•

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\varphi_{\vec{k}}(\xi)\,d\xi=0
and
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}|\Delta\varphi_{\vec{k}}(\xi)|^{2}\,d\xi=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}|\nabla\varphi_{\vec{k}}(\xi)|^{2}\,d\xi=1
.

Next, let
\psi_{\vec{k}}=\Delta\varphi_{\vec{k}}
and define

W_{\vec{k}}(\xi)=\psi_{\vec{k}}(\xi)\vec{k},\quad U_{\vec{k}}(\xi)=\vec{k}\times\nabla\varphi_{\vec{k}}(\xi),

(2.19)

so that
3
3

3

Here we use the vector calculus identity
\mathop{\rm curl}\nolimits(F\times G)=F\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits G-G\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits F+(G\cdot\nabla)F-(F\cdot\nabla)G
.

\mathop{\rm curl}\nolimits U_{\vec{k}}=W_{\vec{k}}
and, for any
a_{\vec{k}}
,
\vec{k}\in\Lambda
, the vector field
W=\sum_{\vec{k}\in\Lambda}a_{\vec{k}}W_{\vec{k}}
satisfies

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int W\,d\xi=0,\quad\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int W\otimes W\,d\xi=\sum_{\vec{k}\in\Lambda}a_{\vec{k}}^{2}\vec{k}\otimes\vec{k}.

Further, we define
H_{\vec{k}}=H_{\vec{k}}(\xi)
to be the antisymmetric zero-mean matrix with the property that for any
v\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
we have
H_{\vec{k}}v=-U_{\vec{k}}\times v
.

The following lemma (originating in the work of Nash
[
Nas54
]
) is crucial:

Lemma 2.3
.

For any compact set
\mathcal{N}\subset\mathcal{S}^{3\times 3}_{+}
there exists a finite
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Q}^{3}
such that there exists smooth functions
a_{\vec{k}}:\mathcal{N}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
with

\sum_{\vec{k}\in\Lambda}a_{\vec{k}}^{2}(R)\vec{k}\otimes\vec{k}=R\quad\textrm{ for any }R\in\mathcal{N}.

(2.20)

The corresponding vector field
W=W(R,\xi)=\sum_{\vec{k}\in\Lambda}a_{\vec{k}}(R)W_{\vec{k}}(\xi)
is called a Mikado flow. In the following we will fix
\mathcal{N}:=B_{1/2}(\mathrm{Id})
, the metric ball of radius
1/2
around the identity matrix in
\mathcal{S}^{3\times 3}_{+}
and denote the corresponding Mikado vector field by
W=W(R,\xi)
.

Following
[
BLSV19
]
the new perturbation is then defined as

w_{q+1}=\frac{1}{\lambda_{q+1}}\mathop{\rm curl}\nolimits\left[\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\sum_{\vec{k}\in\Lambda}\eta_{i}\sigma_{q}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}a_{\vec{k}}(\tilde{R}_{q,i})\nabla\Phi_{i}^{T}U_{\vec{k}}(\lambda_{q+1}\Phi_{i})\right]\,,

(2.21)

where

•

\eta_{i}=\eta_{i}(x,t)
,
i\in\mathbb{N}
, are smooth nonnegative cutoff functions with pairwise disjoint supports such that

\|\partial_{t}^{m}\nabla^{n}_{x}\eta_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C_{n,m}\tau_{q}^{-m},\quad\sum_{i}\eta_{i}^{2}(x,t)=\bar{\eta}^{2}(x,\tau_{q}^{-1}t)\,,

(2.22)

where
\bar{\eta}=\bar{\eta}(x,t)
is a (universal) function,
1
-periodic in
t
, such that

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\bar{\eta}^{2}(x,t)\,dx=c_{0},\quad\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{0}^{1}\bar{\eta}^{2}(x,t)\,dt=c_{1}

for some universal constants
c_{0},c_{1}>0
.

•

\sigma_{q}=\sigma_{q}(t)
is a positive scalar function with the property

|\sigma_{q}(t)-c_{1}^{-1}\delta_{q+1}|\leq C\delta_{q+1}(\lambda_{q}^{-\gamma_{E}}+\lambda_{q}^{-\gamma_{R}}+\lambda_{q}^{-(b-1)\beta}),\quad|\partial_{t}\sigma_{q}(t)|\leq C\delta_{q+1}\tau_{q}^{-1}.

(2.23)

•

The maps
\Phi_{i}=\Phi_{i}(x,t)
are the volume-preserving diffeomorphisms defined as the inverse flow map of the velocity field
\bar{u}_{q}
, which satisfy for
(x,t)\in\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits\eta_{i}
:

\begin{split}&\|\nabla\Phi_{i}-\mathrm{Id}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\lambda_{q}^{-\gamma_{T}},\\
&\|\nabla\Phi_{i}(x,t)\|_{C^{n}}+\|(\nabla\Phi_{i})^{-1}(x,t)\|_{C^{n}}\leq C_{n}\ell_{q}^{-n},\quad\|\nabla^{n}D_{t}\nabla\Phi_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C_{n}\delta_{q}^{1/2}\lambda_{q}\ell_{q}^{-n},\end{split}

(2.24)

where
D_{t}=\partial_{t}+\bar{u}_{q}\cdot\nabla
.

•

\tilde{R}_{q,i}=\tilde{R}_{q,i}(x,t)
,
i\in\mathbb{N}
is given by

\tilde{R}_{q,i}=\nabla\Phi_{i}(\mathrm{Id}-\sigma_{q}^{-1}\mathring{\bar{R}}_{q})\nabla\Phi_{i}^{T}

(2.25)

and satisfies

\|\tilde{R}_{q,i}\|_{C^{n}}\leq C_{n}\ell_{q}^{-n},\quad\|\nabla^{n}D_{t}\tilde{R}_{q,i}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C_{n}\tau_{q}^{-1}\ell_{q}^{-n}.

(2.26)

These properties will be obtained in Section
7.3
, where we will conclude the proof of Proposition
2.1
.

2.2.
Anomalous dissipation via iterative stages

In this section we present the building block for our construction of the advected scalar.
We define an additional
q
-dependent parameter which plays a key role in this paper:

\kappa_{q}:=\lambda_{q}^{-\theta},\quad\theta=\frac{2b}{b+1}(1+\beta)\,.

(2.27)

Using (
2.3
) it is easy to verify the recursive identity

\kappa_{q}=\frac{\delta_{q+1}}{\lambda_{q+1}^{2}\kappa_{q+1}}.

(2.28)

In turn, this identity indicates that we can think of
\kappa_{q}
as having physical dimension of diffusion coefficient
L^{2}T^{-1}
.

We consider the sequence of advection-diffusion equations on
\mathbb{T}^{3}\times[0,T]
:

\begin{split}\partial_{t}\rho_{q}+u_{q}\cdot\nabla\rho_{q}&=\kappa_{q}\Delta\rho_{q}\,,\\
\rho_{q}|_{t=0}&=\rho_{in}\,,\end{split}

(2.29)

where
u_{q}
is the sequence of velocity fields obtained in Section
2.1
via Proposition
2.1
.
We are interested in comparing the cumulative dissipation for subsequent values of
q
, given by

D_{q}:=\kappa_{q}\int_{0}^{T}\|\nabla\rho_{q}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,dt=\frac{1}{2}(\|\rho_{in}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}-\|\rho_{q}(T)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}).

(2.30)

The main result of this section is

Proposition 2.4
.

Within the setting of Proposition
2.1
let us assume in addition that
\beta,b
and
\gamma_{L},\gamma_{T},\gamma_{R}
satisfy

\gamma_{T}<\frac{b-1}{b+1}(1-(2b+1)\beta)<\gamma_{R}+\gamma_{T},

(2.31)

2\gamma_{L}<\frac{b-1}{b+1}(1+\beta).

(2.32)

Then there exists
\tilde{N}\in\mathbb{N}
and
\gamma>0
such that for any sufficiently small
\alpha>0
there exists
a_{0}\gg 1
with the following property:

For any initial datum
\rho_{in}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3})
with
\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\rho_{in}\,dx=0
such that

\|\rho_{in}\|_{H^{n}}\leq\lambda_{q}^{n}\min\{D_{q}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}},D_{q+1}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\}\qquad\textrm{ for }1\leq n\leq\tilde{N},

(2.33)

we have

|D_{q+1}-D_{q}|\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\lambda_{q}^{-\gamma}D_{q}

(2.34)

and

\sup_{t\leq T}\|\rho_{q+1}(t)-\rho_{q}(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq\lambda_{q}^{-2\gamma}D_{q}.

(2.35)

Proof.

We start by fixing
\gamma,\alpha>0
. The exponent
\gamma>0
is determined by the inequalities

\begin{split}\gamma=\tfrac{1}{4}\min\Bigl\{\gamma_{L},\gamma_{T},\gamma_{R},\gamma_{E},(b-1)\beta,(b-1)\theta,\gamma_{T}+\gamma_{R}+(2b-1)\beta+1-\theta,\\
\frac{b-1}{b+1}(1-(2b+1)\beta)-\gamma_{T}\Bigr\}.\end{split}

(2.36)

We remark that the last condition in the first line above can be satisfied because of the right inequality in (
2.31
) and the definition (
2.27
) of
\theta
. In turn,
\alpha
is then chosen sufficiently small so that

\alpha(1+\gamma_{L})+2\gamma+\theta<\gamma_{T}+\gamma_{R}+(2b-1)\beta+1.

(2.37)

We also fix

\begin{split}\tilde{N}\geq\max\Bigl\{2\left(\frac{b-1}{b+1}\big(1-(2b+1)\beta\big)-\gamma_{T}\right)^{-1},\,1+\frac{2b(b-1)(1+\beta)}{\gamma_{T}(b+1)}\Bigr\},\end{split}

(2.38)

where the first lower bound is given by
N_{h}
in (
5.12
) and the second lower bound is given by
N
to validate (A1) in Section
6
. Now we will show the stability estimate
(
2.34
) in several steps, via consecutive stability estimates.

Step 1: Preparation (
\rho_{q+1}\leadsto\tilde{\rho}_{q+1}
)

We start with the equation

\begin{split}\partial_{t}\rho_{q+1}+u_{q+1}\cdot\nabla\rho_{q+1}&=\kappa_{q+1}\Delta\rho_{q+1}\,,\\
\rho_{q+1}|_{t=0}&=\rho_{in},\end{split}

(2.39)

and recall the form
u_{q+1}=\bar{u}_{q}+w_{q+1}
of the advecting field
u_{q+1}
, where
w_{q+1}
is given in (
2.21
). Then we make use of the linear algebra identity
(Au)\times(Av)=\textrm{cof }A(u\times v)=(\det A)A^{-T}(u\times v)
for any
3\times 3
matrix
A
and vectors
u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. From this we deduce that if
H
is the antisymmetric matrix such that
Hv=-u\times v
and
\det A=1
, then
\tilde{H}=A^{-1}HA^{-T}
is the antisymmetric matrix such that
\tilde{H}v=-(A^{T}u)\times v
. In particular, using the antisymmetric matrix-valued functions
H_{\vec{k}}(\xi)
introduced above in Section
2.1.4
, we deduce

(\nabla\Phi^{-1}_{i}H_{\vec{k}}\nabla\Phi^{-T}_{i})v=-(\nabla\Phi_{i}^{T}U_{\vec{k}})\times v.

Therefore, using the identity
\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits(z\times\nabla\rho)=(\mathop{\rm curl}\nolimits z)\cdot\nabla\rho
, we can write equation (
2.39
) equivalently as

\begin{split}\partial_{t}\rho_{q+1}+\bar{u}_{q}\cdot\nabla\rho_{q+1}&=\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits A_{q+1}\nabla\rho_{q+1}\,,\\
\rho_{q+1}|_{t=0}&=\rho_{in}\,,\end{split}

(2.40)

where the elliptic matrix
A_{q+1}=A_{q+1}(x,t)
is defined as

A_{q+1}(x,t)=\kappa_{q+1}\mathrm{Id}+\sum_{i}\nabla\Phi_{i}^{-1}(x,t)\frac{\eta_{i}(x,t)}{\lambda_{q+1}}H^{(i)}(x,t,\lambda_{q+1}\Phi_{i})\nabla\Phi_{i}^{-T}(x,t)\,,

(2.41)

and

\displaystyle H^{(i)}(x,t,\xi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\vec{k}\in\Lambda}\sigma_{q}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}(t)a_{\vec{k}}(\tilde{R}_{q,i}(x,t))H_{\vec{k}}(\lambda_{q+1}\xi)\,.

Let
(\tilde{\eta}_{i})_{i}
be a partition of unity such that
\tilde{\eta}_{i}\eta_{i}=\eta_{i}
,
\tilde{\eta}_{i}\eta_{j}=0
for
j\neq i
and satisfying estimates of the same type as (
2.22
):

\|\partial_{t}^{m}\nabla^{n}_{x}\tilde{\eta}_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\tilde{C}_{n,m}\tau_{q}^{-m}.

(2.42)

Define the elliptic matrix

\tilde{A}_{q+1}(x,t)=\sum_{i}\tilde{\eta}_{i}(x,t)\nabla\Phi_{i}^{-1}(x,t)\left[\kappa_{q+1}\mathrm{Id}+\frac{\eta_{i}(x,t)}{\lambda_{q+1}}H^{(i)}(x,t,\lambda_{q+1}\Phi_{i}(x,t))\right]\nabla\Phi_{i}^{-T}(x,t)\,.

(2.43)

The estimate (
2.24
) implies, since
\tilde{\eta}_{i}
is a partition of unity

\|A_{q+1}-\tilde{A}_{q+1}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\kappa_{q+1}\sum_{i}\tilde{\eta}_{i}\|\nabla\Phi_{i}^{-1}\nabla\Phi_{i}^{-T}-\mathrm{Id}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\kappa_{q+1}\lambda_{q}^{-\gamma_{T}}.

(2.44)

Therefore we can compare the cumulative dissipation in (
2.40
) with that in

\begin{split}\partial_{t}\tilde{\rho}_{q+1}+\bar{u}_{q}\cdot\nabla\tilde{\rho}_{q+1}&=\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits\tilde{A}_{q+1}\nabla\tilde{\rho}_{q+1}\,,\\
\tilde{\rho}_{q+1}|_{t=0}&=\rho_{in}\,.\end{split}

(2.45)

More precisely, assuming that
a_{0}
is sufficiently large we can ensure
C\lambda_{q}^{-\gamma_{T}}\leq\tfrac{1}{10}\lambda_{q}^{-\nicefrac{{\gamma_{T}}}{{2}}}
, and then apply
Proposition
4.3
with
\varepsilon=\tfrac{1}{10}\lambda_{q}^{-\nicefrac{{\gamma_{T}}}{{4}}}\leq\tfrac{1}{2}
to conclude

\displaystyle\sup_{t\leq T}\|\rho_{q+1}(t)-\tilde{\rho}_{q+1}(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\lambda_{q}^{-\gamma_{T}}\left|\|\rho_{in}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}-\|\tilde{\rho}_{q+1}(T)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right|,

\displaystyle\left|\|\rho_{q+1}(T)\|^{2}_{L^{2}}-\|\tilde{\rho}_{q+1}(T)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right|\leq

\displaystyle\lambda_{q}^{-\nicefrac{{\gamma_{T}}}{{2}}}\left|\|\rho_{in}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}-\|\tilde{\rho}_{q+1}(T)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right|.

We may also write this as

\left|D_{q+1}-\tilde{D}_{q+1}\right|\leq\lambda_{q}^{-\nicefrac{{\gamma_{T}}}{{2}}}\tilde{D}_{q+1}\leq\lambda_{q}^{-{2\gamma}}\tilde{D}_{q+1},

(2.46)

where
\tilde{D}_{q+1}=\tfrac{1}{2}\left|\|\rho_{in}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}-\|\tilde{\rho}_{q+1}(T)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right|
.

Step 2: Spatial homogenization (
\tilde{\rho}_{q+1}\leadsto\bar{\rho}_{q}^{(1)}
)

In the second step, we use estimates of quantitative homogenization and an explicit formula for the corrector, to replace (
2.45
) by the homogenized equation

\begin{split}\partial_{t}\bar{\rho}_{q}^{(1)}+\bar{u}_{q}\cdot\nabla\bar{\rho}^{(1)}_{q}&=\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits\bar{A}_{q}\nabla\bar{\rho}^{(1)}_{q}\,,\\
\bar{\rho}_{q}^{(1)}|_{t=0}&=\rho_{in}\,.\end{split}

(2.47)

The homogenized elliptic coefficient is given, like in classical homogenization theory, by

\bar{A}_{q}(x,t)=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int\tilde{A}_{q+1}(x,t,\xi)\Big(\mathrm{Id}+\sum_{i}\eta_{i}(x,t)\nabla\Phi_{i}^{T}(x,t)\nabla_{\xi}\chi_{i}^{T}(x,t,\xi)\Big)\,d\xi\,

with corrector
\chi_{i}:\mathbb{T}^{3}\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
chosen in such a way, that in the homogenization Ansatz in the presence of a macroscopic drift (cf.
[
MPP85
,
HYW04
]
)

\rho(x,t)=\bar{\rho}(x,t)+\frac{1}{\lambda}\sum_{i}\eta_{i}(x,t)\chi_{i}\big(x,t,\lambda\Phi_{i}(x,t)\big)\cdot\nabla\bar{\rho}(x,t)+\tilde{\rho}(x,t)

\tilde{\rho}
will be lower order. We will show below in Section
5
that, because of the special structure of the oscillating Mikado vector field
w_{q+1}
,
\chi
can be defined
explicitly
by

\chi_{i}(x,t,\xi)=\frac{\sigma_{q}^{1/2}(t)}{\kappa_{q+1}\lambda_{q+1}}\nabla\Phi_{i}^{-1}(x,t)\sum_{\vec{k}}a_{\vec{k}}\big(\tilde{R}_{q,i}(x,t)\big)\varphi_{\vec{k}}(\xi)\vec{k}\,.

(2.48)

Using the properties of Mikado flows in Section
2.1.4
, this formula allows us to obtain an explicit expression for
\bar{A}_{q+1}
. Indeed, since

H^{(i)}

satisfies
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int H^{(i)}\,d\xi=0
, we have

\bar{A}_{q}=\kappa_{q+1}\sum_{i}\tilde{\eta}_{i}\nabla\Phi_{i}^{-1}\nabla\Phi_{i}^{-T}+\frac{1}{\lambda_{q+1}}\sum_{i}\eta_{i}^{2}\nabla\Phi_{i}^{-1}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int H^{(i)}(\nabla_{\xi}\chi_{i})^{T}\,d\xi.

On the other hand, using (
2.48
) and the definition of
H^{(i)}(x,t,\xi)
,

\displaystyle H^{(i)}(\nabla_{\xi}\chi_{i})^{T}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma_{q}}{\lambda_{q+1}\kappa_{q+1}}\sum_{\vec{k}\in\Lambda}a_{\vec{k}}^{2}(\tilde{R}_{q,i})H_{\vec{k}}(\nabla\varphi_{\vec{k}}\otimes(\nabla\Phi_{i}^{-1}\vec{k}))

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma_{q}}{\lambda_{q+1}\kappa_{q+1}}\sum_{\vec{k}\in\Lambda}a_{\vec{k}}^{2}(\tilde{R}_{q,i})|\nabla\varphi_{\vec{k}}|^{2}(\vec{k}\otimes\vec{k})\nabla\Phi_{i}^{-T},

where we used the definition of
H_{\vec{k}}(\xi)
to deduce

H_{\vec{k}}\nabla\varphi_{\vec{k}}=-(\vec{k}\times\nabla\varphi_{\vec{k}})\times\nabla\varphi_{\vec{k}}=|\nabla\varphi_{\vec{k}}|^{2}\vec{k}.

Using the normalization in the definition of Mikado flows in Section
2.1.4
as well as Lemma
2.3
, we conclude

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int H^{(i)}(\nabla_{\xi}\chi_{i})^{T}\,d\xi=\frac{\sigma_{q}}{\lambda_{q+1}\kappa_{q+1}}\tilde{R}_{q,i}\nabla\Phi_{i}^{-T}\,.

Hence

\begin{split}\bar{A}_{q}(x,t)&=\kappa_{q+1}\sum_{i}\tilde{\eta}_{i}\nabla\Phi^{-1}_{i}\nabla\Phi_{i}^{-T}+\frac{\sigma_{q}}{\lambda_{q+1}^{2}\kappa_{q+1}}\sum_{i}\eta_{i}^{2}\nabla\Phi_{i}^{-1}\tilde{R}_{q,i}\nabla\Phi_{i}^{-T}\\
&=\kappa_{q+1}\sum_{i}\tilde{\eta}_{i}\nabla\Phi^{-1}_{i}\nabla\Phi_{i}^{-T}+\sigma_{q}\delta^{-1}_{q+1}\kappa_{q}\bar{\eta}^{2}(x,\tau_{q}^{-1}t)(\mathrm{Id}-\sigma_{q}^{-1}\mathring{\bar{R}}_{q})\\
&=\kappa_{q+1}\sum_{i}\tilde{\eta}_{i}\nabla\Phi^{-1}_{i}\nabla\Phi_{i}^{-T}+\kappa_{q}\delta^{-1}_{q+1}\sigma_{q}\bar{\eta}^{2}(x,\tau_{q}^{-1}t)\mathrm{Id}-\kappa_{q}\delta^{-1}_{q+1}\bar{\eta}^{2}(x,\tau_{q}^{-1}t)\mathring{\bar{R}}_{q},\end{split}

(2.49)

where for the second line we have used (
2.25
) and (
2.28
), (
2.22
).
Let

\tilde{\kappa}_{q}(x,t):=\kappa_{q+1}+\kappa_{q}c_{1}^{-1}\bar{\eta}^{2}(x,\tau_{q}^{-1}t),

(2.50)

Therefore we have, using that
(\tilde{\eta}_{i})_{i}
is a partition of unity

\bar{A}_{q}-\tilde{\kappa}_{q}\mathrm{Id}=\kappa_{q+1}\sum_{i}\tilde{\eta}_{i}(\nabla\Phi_{i}^{-1}\nabla\Phi_{i}^{-T}-\mathrm{Id})+\kappa_{q}(\delta_{q+1}^{-1}\sigma_{q}-c_{1}^{-1})\bar{\eta}^{2}\mathrm{Id}-\kappa_{q}\delta_{q+1}^{-1}\bar{\eta}^{2}\mathring{\bar{R}}_{q},

(2.51)

so, using (
2.24
), (
2.23
), (
2.14
)

|\bar{A}_{q}-\tilde{\kappa}_{q}\mathrm{Id}|\leq C\kappa_{q+1}\sum_{i}\tilde{\eta}_{i}\lambda_{q}^{-\gamma_{T}}+C\kappa_{q}\bar{\eta}^{2}(\lambda_{q}^{-\gamma_{E}}+\lambda_{q}^{-\gamma_{R}}+\lambda_{q}^{-(b-1)\beta})+C\kappa_{q}\bar{\eta}^{2}\delta_{q+1}^{-1}\mathring{\delta}_{q+1}\ell_{q}^{-2\alpha}.

(2.52)

Using now that by definition
\mathring{\delta}_{q+1}=\delta_{q+1}\lambda_{q}^{-\gamma_{R}}
, the fact that we can choose
\alpha
arbitrarily small, and the definition (
2.50
) of
\tilde{\kappa}_{q}
, we see that (
2.52
) yields for all
(x,t)

|\bar{A}_{q}(x,t)-\tilde{\kappa}_{q}(x,t)\mathrm{Id}|\leq C\tilde{\kappa}_{q}(x,t)\lambda_{q}^{-\min\{\gamma_{T},\gamma_{R},\gamma_{E},(b-1)\beta\}}\leq C\tilde{\kappa}_{q}(x,t)\lambda_{q}^{-2\gamma}

(2.53)

for some fixed constant
C
, where the latter inequality of (
2.53
) holds via the choice (
2.36
) of
\gamma
.
Then, as in Step 1 we choose
a_{0}
sufficiently large to ensure that
C\lambda_{q}^{-2\gamma}\leq\tfrac{1}{10}\lambda_{q}^{-\frac{3}{2}\gamma}
. In particular, we may bound pointwise

\frac{1}{2}\tilde{\kappa}_{q}\mathrm{Id}\leq\bar{A}_{q}\leq 2\tilde{\kappa}_{q}\mathrm{Id}.

(2.54)

We intend now to apply Corollary
5.4
of Section
5
, with
\gamma
given in (
2.36
) and the following choices

\begin{split}\rho=&\tilde{\rho}_{q+1},\\
\bar{\rho}=&\bar{\rho}_{q}^{(1)},\\
u=&\bar{u}_{q},\\
\tilde{R}_{i}=&\tilde{R}_{q,i},\end{split}\quad\quad\quad\begin{split}\kappa=&\kappa_{q+1},\\
\lambda=&\lambda_{q+1},\\
\ell=&\ell_{q},\\
\tau=&\tau_{q},\end{split}\quad\quad\quad\begin{split}\delta=&\delta_{q+1},\\
\sigma=&\sigma_{q},\\
\bar{\kappa}=&\tilde{\kappa}_{q}.\end{split}

(2.55)

In order to proceed, we need to verify the assumptions gathered in Section
5.1.1
(for Proposition
5.1
) and the assumption (
5.26
) (needed additionally for Corollary
5.4
) for these choices of parameters. First of all, direct computations yield (
5.8
)-(
5.12
) from (
2.31
), (
2.32
), (
2.36
), and (
2.38
). The remaining assumptions of Section
5.1.1
follow from the construction of the vector field in Section
2.1
. More precisely, assumptions involving
\sigma
, i.e. (
5.13
) and (
5.20
), follow from (
2.23
). Assumptions on
\Phi_{i}
follow from (
2.24
) and (
2.7
). The assumption (
5.15
) is given by (
2.54
) and the assumption (
5.16
) on
u
is given by (
2.13
). The assumption (
5.17
) involving
R
follows from (
2.26
) and the fact that the functions
a_{\vec{k}}
and their derivatives are bounded by absolute constants. The required properties of functions
\eta_{i}
and
\tilde{\eta}_{i}
follow from (
2.22
), (
2.42
).
In turn, the explicit formula (
5.6
) can then be used to verify assumption (
5.19
). Finally, (
5.26
) follows from (
2.33
).

Summarizing, Proposition
5.1
and Corollaries
5.2
,
5.3
and
5.4
are applicable. We obtain:

\displaystyle\sup_{t\leq T}\|\tilde{\rho}_{q+1}(t)-\bar{\rho}_{q}^{(1)}(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim

\displaystyle\lambda_{q}^{-4\gamma}\left|\|\rho_{in}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}-\|\bar{\rho}^{(1)}_{q}(T)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right|,

\displaystyle\left|\|\tilde{\rho}_{q+1}(T)\|^{2}_{L^{2}}-\|\bar{\rho}_{q}^{(1)}(T)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right|\lesssim

\displaystyle\lambda_{q}^{-2\gamma}\left|\|\rho_{in}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}-\|\bar{\rho}^{(1)}_{q}(T)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right|\,.

Thus, using
D_{q}^{(1)}=\tfrac{1}{2}\left|\|\rho_{in}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}-\|\bar{\rho}^{(1)}_{q}(T)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right|
,

\begin{split}\sup_{t\leq T}\|\tilde{\rho}_{q+1}(t)-\bar{\rho}_{q}^{(1)}(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq&\lambda_{q}^{-3\gamma}D_{q}^{(1)},\\
\left|\tilde{D}_{q+1}-D_{q}^{(1)}\right|\leq C\lambda_{q}^{-2\gamma}D_{q}^{(1)}\leq&\lambda_{q}^{-\frac{3}{2}\gamma}D_{q}^{(1)}.\end{split}

(2.56)

Step 3: Diagonal reduction (
\bar{\rho}_{q}^{(1)}\leadsto\bar{\rho}_{q}^{(2)}
)

Using once more (
2.53
), we now apply Proposition
4.3
with

\varepsilon=\tfrac{1}{10}\lambda_{q}^{-\frac{3}{2}\gamma}
to conclude

\begin{split}\sup_{t\leq T}\|\bar{\rho}_{q}^{(2)}(t)-\bar{\rho}_{q}^{(1)}(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq\lambda_{q}^{-3\gamma}D_{q}^{(2)},\\
\left|D_{q}^{(2)}-D_{q}^{(1)}\right|\leq\lambda_{q}^{-\frac{3}{2}\gamma}D_{q}^{(2)},\end{split}

(2.57)

where
D_{q}^{(i)}=\tfrac{1}{2}\left|\|\rho_{in}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}-\|\bar{\rho}^{(i)}_{q}(T)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right|
and
\bar{\rho}^{(2)}_{q}
is the solution of

\begin{split}\partial_{t}\bar{\rho}^{(2)}_{q}+\bar{u}_{q}\cdot\nabla\bar{\rho}^{(2)}_{q}&=\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits\tilde{\kappa}_{q}\nabla\bar{\rho}^{(2)}_{q}\,,\\
\bar{\rho}_{q}^{(2)}|_{t=0}&=\rho_{in}\,.\end{split}

(2.58)

Step 4: Time averaging (
\bar{\rho}_{q}^{(2)}\leadsto\bar{\rho}_{q}^{(3)}
)

The advection-diffusion equation (
2.58
) has two different characteristic time-scales: the advective time-scale
\|\nabla\bar{u}_{q}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{-1}
and the time-scale
\tau_{q}
given by the time-oscillatory behaviour of the ellipticity coefficient
\tilde{\kappa}_{q}(x,t)
given in (
2.50
), with the relationship given by (
2.7
):
\|\nabla\bar{u}_{q}\|_{L^{\infty}}\tau_{q}\leq M\lambda_{q}^{-\gamma_{T}}
. Let us now invoke Proposition
6.1
with the following choices
(on the right-hand side there are ingredients of the current construction, which define the objects appearing in Proposition
6.1
)

\eta:=c_{1}^{-1}\bar{\eta}^{2},\quad\kappa_{0}:=\kappa_{q+1},\quad\kappa_{1}:=\kappa_{q},\quad\mu:=\delta_{q}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q},\quad\tau:=\tau_{q},\quad u:=\bar{u}_{q}.

Proposition
6.1
requires assumptions (A1)-(A4). The assumption (A1) follows from
\tau\mu=\lambda_{q}^{-\gamma_{T}}
(valid in view of (
2.7
)), and the second lower bound for
\tilde{N}
in (
2.38
); observe that the resulting
N
of (A1) does not depend on
q
. The assumptions (A2)-(A3) follow from assumptions (
2.31
), (
2.32
), (
2.33
), (
2.13
),
b>1
, as well as (
2.7
). Control of the cutoff-functions in (
2.22
) implies (A4).
Proposition
6.1
then yields

\displaystyle\sup_{t\leq T}\|\bar{\rho}^{(3)}_{q}(t)-\bar{\rho}^{(2)}_{q}(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq C\lambda_{q}^{-2\gamma_{T}}(D^{(2)}_{q}+D^{(3)}_{q}),

\left|\|\bar{\rho}^{(3)}_{q}(T)\|^{2}_{L^{2}}-\|\bar{\rho}^{(2)}_{q}(T)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right|\leq C\lambda_{q}^{-\gamma_{T}}(D^{(2)}_{q}+D^{(3)}_{q}),

(2.59)

where

D^{(i)}_{q}=\tfrac{1}{2}\left|\|\rho_{in}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}-\|\bar{\rho}^{(i)}_{q}(T)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right|

and
\bar{\rho}^{(3)}_{q}
is the solution of

\begin{split}\partial_{t}\bar{\rho}^{(3)}_{q}+\bar{u}_{q}\cdot\nabla\bar{\rho}^{(3)}_{q}&=(\kappa_{q+1}+\kappa_{q})\Delta\bar{\rho}^{(3)}_{q}\,,\\
\bar{\rho}_{q}^{(3)}|_{t=0}&=\rho_{in}\,.\end{split}

(2.60)

Choosing
a_{0}
sufficiently large, we may ensure that
C\lambda_{q}^{-\gamma_{T}}<\tfrac{1}{4}\lambda_{q}^{-\frac{3}{2}\gamma}
in (
2.59
), from which we can then conclude

\left|\frac{D_{q}^{(2)}}{D_{q}^{(3)}}-1\right|\leq\frac{1}{4}\lambda_{q}^{-\frac{3}{2}\gamma}\left(1+\frac{D_{q}^{(2)}}{D_{q}^{(3)}}\right).

We deduce

\left|\frac{D_{q}^{(2)}}{D_{q}^{(3)}}-1\right|\leq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{q}^{-\frac{3}{2}\gamma}.

(2.61)

Step 5: Gluing estimate (
\bar{\rho}_{q}^{(3)}\leadsto\rho_{q}
)

Finally, we compare (
2.60
) to (
2.29
) by using the estimate (
2.17
). Indeed, using potentials for velocities (cf. (
2.18
)) we can write (
2.60
) as

\begin{split}\partial_{t}\bar{\rho}^{(3)}_{q}+u_{q}\cdot\nabla\bar{\rho}^{(3)}_{q}&=\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits\left(\kappa_{q}\nabla\bar{\rho}^{(3)}_{q}+\kappa_{q+1}\nabla\bar{\rho}^{(3)}_{q}+(z_{q}-\bar{z}_{q})\times\nabla\bar{\rho}^{(3)}_{q}\right)\,,\\
\bar{\rho}_{q}^{(3)}|_{t=0}&=\rho_{in}\,.\end{split}

On the other hand (
2.17
), (
2.7
) and our choice of parameters (
2.37
), (
2.36
) imply

\|z_{q}-\bar{z}_{q}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\tau_{q}\delta_{q+1}\lambda_{q}^{-\gamma_{R}+\alpha(1+\gamma_{L})}\leq C\kappa_{q}\lambda_{q}^{-2\gamma}\,,\quad\kappa_{q+1}=\lambda_{q}^{-(b-1)\theta}\kappa_{q}\leq\kappa_{q}\lambda_{q}^{-4\gamma}.

(2.62)

A final application of Proposition
4.3
, assuming
a_{0}
is sufficiently large to absorb the constant, leads to

\displaystyle\sup_{t\leq T}\|\bar{\rho}^{(3)}_{q}(t)-\rho_{q}(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{q}^{-3\gamma}\left|\|\rho_{in}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}-\|\rho_{q}(T)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right|,

\displaystyle\left|\|\bar{\rho}^{(3)}_{q}(T)\|^{2}_{L^{2}}-\|\rho_{q}(T)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{q}^{-\frac{3}{2}\gamma}\left|\|\rho_{in}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}-\|\rho_{q}(T)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right|,

where
\rho_{q}
is the solution of (
2.29
), equivalently

\begin{split}\sup_{t\leq T}\|\bar{\rho}^{(3)}_{q}(t)-\rho_{q}(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq\lambda_{q}^{-3\gamma}D_{q}\,,\\
\left|D_{q}^{(3)}-D_{q}\right|\leq\lambda_{q}^{-\frac{3}{2}\gamma}D_{q}\,.\end{split}

(2.63)

Final estimate

Overall, we see that after the five stability steps above, we achieve the estimate

\frac{D_{q+1}}{D_{q}}=\frac{D_{q+1}}{\tilde{D}_{q+1}}\frac{\tilde{D}_{q+1}}{D_{q}^{(1)}}\frac{D_{q}^{(1)}}{D_{q}^{(2)}}\frac{D_{q}^{(2)}}{D_{q}^{(3)}}\frac{D_{q}^{(3)}}{D_{q}}\geq\left(1-\lambda_{q}^{-\frac{3}{2}\gamma}\right)^{5}D_{q}\geq\left(1-\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{q}^{-\gamma}\right)D_{q},

(2.64)

where we have again assumed
a_{0}
is sufficiently large to absorb the exponent
5
and gain the factor
\frac{1}{2}
at the expense of decreasing
\frac{3}{2}\gamma
to
\gamma
.
The estimate (
2.64
) together with the analogous estimate from above yields (
2.34
). Similarly we obtain (
2.35
).
The proof of Proposition
2.4
is concluded.
∎

2.3.
Construction of the vector field - h-principle

In Section
2.1
we detailed the main iteration scheme for producing Hölder-continuous weak solutions of the Euler equations. What remains is to produce an initial vector field and associated Reynolds tensor, which satisfies the inductive assumptions (
2.4
)-(
2.6
) for
some

q\in\mathbb{N}
.
To this end we recall that a smooth strict subsolution to the Euler equations is a smooth triple
(\bar{u},\bar{p},\bar{R})
on
\mathbb{T}^{3}\times[0,T]
solving the Euler-Reynolds system (
2.1
) with the normalizations
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\bar{u}\,dx=0
,
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\bar{p}\,dx=0
, such that
\bar{R}(x,t)
is uniformly positive definite on
\mathbb{T}^{3}\times[0,T]
. The energy associated with a subsolution is (cf.
[
DS17
,
DRS21
,
BLSV19
]
)

e(t)=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}(|\bar{u}|^{2}+\mathrm{tr\,}\bar{R})\,dx.

(2.65)

We have the following statement, which is a variant of
[
DS17
, Proposition 3.1]
, see also
[
BLSV19
, Theorem 7.1]
and
[
DRS21
, Proposition 4.1]
.

Proposition 2.5
.

Let
(\bar{u},\bar{p},\bar{R})
be a smooth strict subsolution with energy
e
. Let the parameters
M,\bar{e}
,
\beta,b,\gamma_{T},\gamma_{R},\gamma_{E},\alpha>0
and
a_{0}\gg 1
be as in Proposition
2.1
, and
(\delta_{q},\lambda_{q})_{q\in\mathbb{N}}
be as in (
2.3
).

Then, there exists
q_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that, for any
q\geq q_{0}
there exists
(u_{q},\mathring{R}_{q})
solving (
2.1
) with (
2.2
) such that (
2.4
)-(
2.6
) hold. Moreover, for any
\alpha\in(0,1)

\displaystyle\|\bar{u}-u_{q}\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\leq\bar{C}\|\bar{R}\|_{C^{0}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\,,

(2.66)

\displaystyle\|\bar{z}-z_{q}\|_{C^{\alpha}}

\displaystyle\leq\bar{C}\|\bar{R}\|_{C^{\alpha}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q}^{-1+\alpha}\,,

(2.67)

where the constant
\bar{C}
only depends on
\alpha
,
\bar{u}
and on
\delta:=\inf_{(x,t)}\frac{\min\{\bar{R}\zeta\cdot\zeta:|\zeta|=1\}}{\mathrm{tr\,}\bar{R}}
.

Proof.

We construct, following
[
DS17
,
DRS21
]
,
u_{q}:=\bar{u}+w
, where
w
is from the perturbation step of Section
7
, but without the need for time-cutoffs. More precisely, we define

\tilde{R}=\bar{R}-\tfrac{1}{3}\delta_{q+1}\mathrm{Id}.

For
q
sufficiently large, we ensure that

\frac{1}{\mathrm{tr\,}\tilde{R}}\tilde{R}\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\delta\mathrm{Id}.

Further, define
\Phi=\Phi(x,t)
to be the inverse flow associated to the vector field
\bar{u}
, i.e. such that
(\partial_{t}+\bar{u}\cdot\nabla)\Phi=0
and
\Phi(x,0)=x
. Since
\bar{u}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{3}\times[0,T])
with
\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits\bar{u}=0
, for every
t
the map
\Phi(\cdot,t)
is a volume-preserving diffeomorphism, and there exists a constant
C
such that

C^{-1}\leq|\nabla\Phi|,|\nabla\Phi^{-1}|\leq C.

Now we apply Lemma
2.3
with

\mathcal{N}:=\left\{R\in\mathcal{S}^{3\times 3}_{+}:\,R=\tfrac{1}{\mathrm{tr\,}S}ASA^{T}\textrm{ where $C^{-1}\leq|A|,|A^{-1}|\leq C$ and $S\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\delta\mathrm{tr\,}S\,\mathrm{Id}$}\right\}\,,

to obtain
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Q}^{3}
and
a_{\vec{k}}
satisfying (
2.20
).
We set (cf. (
7.38
))

\displaystyle\tilde{S}=\frac{1}{\mathrm{tr\,}\tilde{R}}\nabla\Phi\tilde{R}\nabla\Phi^{T},

and observe that
\tilde{S}(x,t)\in\mathcal{N}
. The new perturbation
w
is then defined, in analogy with (
2.21
), as

w=\frac{1}{\lambda}\mathop{\rm curl}\nolimits\left[\sum_{\vec{k}\in\Lambda}(\mathrm{tr\,}\tilde{R})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}a_{\vec{k}}(\tilde{S})\nabla\Phi^{T}U_{\vec{k}}(\lambda\Phi)\right]\,.

(2.68)

The associated Reynolds stress is (cf. (
7.54
))

\mathring{R}_{q}=\mathcal{R}\left[\partial_{t}w+\bar{u}\cdot\nabla w+w\cdot\nabla\bar{u}+\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits(w\otimes w-\tilde{R})\right],

where
\mathcal{R}
is the inverse divergence operator on symmetric
2
-tensors, introduced in
[
DLS13
]
(cf. (
7.20
) in Section
7
).
Since
\bar{R}-\tilde{R}
is a constant multiple of the identity, it easily follows, as in
[
DS17
,
BLSV19
]
, that
u_{q},\mathring{R}_{q}
is a solution of (
2.1
). Moreover, the estimates in
[
DS17
,
BLSV19
]
(see also Section
7.3
) imply

\displaystyle\|\mathring{R}_{q}\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\leq C\lambda^{-1+\alpha}\,,

\displaystyle\|w\|_{C^{n}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\bar{R}\|_{C^{0}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda^{n}\quad\textrm{ for all }n\leq\bar{N}\,,

\displaystyle\left|\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}|u_{q}|^{2}-|\bar{u}|^{2}-\mathrm{tr\,}\tilde{R}\,dx\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\lambda^{-1+\alpha}\,.

Here the constant
C
depends on
(\bar{u},\bar{R})
and on
\mathcal{N}
.
It remains to choose
\lambda
and
q
. We fix an exponent
0<\gamma_{o}
such that

\beta<\gamma_{o}\textrm{ and }\alpha+\gamma_{o}<1-2b\beta-\max\{\gamma_{E},\gamma_{R}\}

and define
\lambda=\lambda_{q}^{1-\gamma_{o}}
(
q
still to be fixed).
Observe that (
2.8
)-(
2.9
) guarantee the existence of such
\gamma_{o}
. Now, validity of (
2.4
)-(
2.6
) follows from

C\lambda^{-1+\alpha}\leq\delta_{q+1}\lambda_{q}^{-\max\{\gamma_{E},\gamma_{R}\}},\textrm{ as well as }C\lambda\leq M\delta_{q}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q},\,\lambda\leq\lambda_{q}.

But by our choice of the exponent
\gamma_{o}
these inequalities are satisfied for
q
sufficiently large.
∎

3.
Proofs of main results

Now we are ready to state our main result in full generality:

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
T_{0}<\infty
. Let
(\bar{u},\bar{p},\bar{R})
be a smooth strict Euler subsolution on
\mathbb{T}^{3}\times[0,T_{0}]
with smooth, strictly positive energy
e:[0,T_{0}]\to\mathbb{R}
. Let
0<\beta<1/3
and
1<b<\min\{\sqrt{\tfrac{3}{2}},\tfrac{1-\beta}{2\beta}\}
. There exists
\bar{C}
, depending only on
\bar{u}
and on
\inf_{(x,t)}\frac{\min\{\bar{R}\zeta\cdot\zeta:|\zeta|=1\}}{\mathrm{tr\,}\bar{R}}
such that, for any
\epsilon>0
, we have:

There exists a weak solution
u\in C^{\beta}(\mathbb{T}^{3}\times[0,T_{0}])
to the Euler equations (
1.3
) with kinetic energy
\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}|u(x,t)|^{2}\,dx=e(t)
for all
t
, such that

\displaystyle\|\bar{u}-u\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\leq\bar{C}\|\bar{R}\|_{C^{0}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\,,

(3.1)

\displaystyle\|\bar{z}-z\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon,

(3.2)

where
\bar{z},z
are potentials for, respectively,
\bar{u}
,
u
(cf. (
2.18
)).

Moreover, there exists
\delta_{0}>0
(depending on the previous choices) such that for any
T\leq T_{0}
and for every non-zero initial datum
\rho_{in}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{3})
with zero mean, the family of unique solutions
\{\rho_{\kappa}\}_{\kappa>0}
to advection-diffusion equation (
1.1
) with velocity field
u
satisfies

\limsup_{\kappa\to 0}\quad\kappa\int_{0}^{T}\|\nabla\rho_{\kappa}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,dt\geq c_{0}\|\rho_{in}\|_{L^{2}}^{2},

(3.3)

where
c_{0}>0
satisfies

c_{0}\geq C(\beta,b)\ell_{in}^{-2}\min\left\{\left(\frac{\ell_{in}}{\ell_{0}}\right)^{\frac{4b}{2-b\theta}}T^{\frac{2b}{2-b\theta}},\delta_{0},\ell_{in}^{2b}\right\}.

(3.4)

where
\ell_{0}:=\sup_{f\in\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{3})}\frac{\|f\|_{L^{2}}}{\|\nabla f\|_{L^{2}}}
,

\ell_{in}:=\frac{\|\rho_{in}\|_{L^{2}}}{\|\nabla\rho_{in}\|_{L^{2}}}
, and
\theta=\frac{2b}{b+1}(1+\beta)
(c.f. (
2.27
))

Before proving Theorem
3.1
, we show how to deduce Theorem
1.1
as well as the density statements in Section
1.2
.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Take
\bar{u}=0
,
\bar{p}=0
and
\bar{R}=\tfrac{1}{3}e(t)\mathrm{Id}
for a strictly positive, smooth function
e
(say, constant) as the strict subsolution in Theorem
3.1
. This generates an Euler solution
u\in C^{\beta}(\mathbb{T}^{3}\times[0,T_{0}])
such that the advection-diffusion equation with velocity
u
exhibits anomalous dissipation (
3.3
). For fixed
u
and
b
the constant (
3.4
) depends only on
\ell_{in},\beta,T
and
\ell_{0}
.
∎

Now we come to the density statements in Section
1.1
.

Strong density

Let
(\bar{u},\bar{p})
be a smooth solution of the Euler equations and consider the strict subsolution
(\bar{u},\bar{p},\bar{R}_{n})
, where
\bar{R}_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\mathrm{Id}
. Then
\inf_{(x,t)}\frac{\min\{\bar{R}\zeta\cdot\zeta:|\zeta|=1\}}{\mathrm{tr\,}\bar{R}}=3
, so that the constant
\bar{C}
of (
3.1
) is uniform in
n
. Therefore, in light of estimate (
3.1
), Theorem
3.1
yields a sequence
u^{n}
of weak solutions to the Euler equations inducing anomalous dissipation, with
u^{n}\to\bar{u}
uniformly.

Weak density

Let
\bar{u}
be an arbitrary smooth divergence-free vector field. We may write, for an arbitrary smooth positive function
\tilde{e}=\tilde{e}(t)
,

\partial_{t}\bar{u}+\bar{u}\cdot\nabla\bar{u}=\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits(\tilde{e}(t)\mathrm{Id}+\mathcal{R}(\partial_{t}\bar{u})+\bar{u}\otimes\bar{u}),

where
\mathcal{R}
is the symmetric antidivergence operator as in the proof of Proposition
2.5
(cf. (
7.20
)). In particular, choosing
\tilde{e}(t)
sufficiently large we may ensure that the symmetric tensor
\bar{R}:=\tilde{e}(t)\mathrm{Id}+\mathcal{R}(\partial_{t}\bar{u})+\bar{u}\otimes\bar{u}
is positive definite, so that
(\bar{u},0,\bar{R})
is a strict subsolution. In light of the estimate (
3.2
), Theorem
3.1
then yields a sequence
u^{n}
of weak solutions to the Euler equations inducing anomalous dissipation, with the corresponding potentials
z^{n}\to\bar{z}
uniformly.

3.1.
Proof of Theorem
3.1

We start with a smooth strict Euler subsolution
(\bar{u},\bar{p},\bar{R})
with strictly positive energy profile
e:[0,T_{0}]\to\mathbb{R}
and
0<\beta<1/3
.

Step 1. Choice of parameters.

We fix
\gamma_{T},\gamma_{R},\gamma_{E}>0
as follows

\gamma_{E}=\gamma_{T}=\gamma_{R}=\frac{b-1}{b(b+1)}(1-(2b+1)\beta),

(3.5)

which implies

\displaystyle\gamma_{T}+b\gamma_{R}

\displaystyle<(b-1)(1-(2b+1)\beta)\,,

(3.6)

\displaystyle\gamma_{T}

\displaystyle<\frac{b-1}{b+1}(1-(2b+1)\beta)\,,

(3.7)

\displaystyle\gamma_{R}+\gamma_{T}

\displaystyle>\frac{b-1}{b+1}(1-(2b+1)\beta)\,.

(3.8)

Note that (
3.6
) gives the requirement (
2.9
) in Proposition
2.1
, whereas (
3.7
) and (
3.8
) are the left and right inequalities in (
2.31
) in Proposition
2.4
.

Having fixed the parameters
e,\beta,b,\gamma_{T},\gamma_{R},\gamma_{E}
, we choose
\gamma_{L}>0
sufficiently small in accordance with the requirements of Propositions
2.1
,
2.4
. This in turn fixes
\bar{N}
via Proposition
2.1
and
\gamma,\tilde{N}
via Propositions
2.4
. Next, let small
\alpha
and then
a_{0}
be chosen according to requirements of Propositions
2.1
,
2.4
. Then, for any
a\geq a_{0}
the two propositions are applicable, with the sequence
\{(\lambda_{q},\delta_{q})\}_{q}
determined according to (
2.3
), i.e.
\lambda_{q}=2\pi\lceil a^{b^{q}}\rceil
and
\delta_{q}=\lambda_{q}^{-2\beta}
. In particular, choosing
a\gg a_{0}
sufficiently large we may ensure

\prod_{q^{\prime}\geq{1}}(1-\tfrac{1}{2}\lambda_{q^{\prime}}^{-\gamma})>\tfrac{1}{2}.

(3.9)

Step 2. Construction of the vector field
u
.
We apply Proposition
2.5
to obtain
(u_{q_{0}},\mathring{R}_{q_{0}})
satisfying the estimates (
2.4
)-(
2.6
) and (
2.66
)- (
2.67
). Then Proposition
2.1
applied inductively for any
q\geq q_{0}
yields the sequence
\{u_{q}\}_{q\geq q_{0}}
. Arguing as in
[
BLSV19
]
, we deduce that
u_{q}\to u
in
C([0,T];C^{\beta^{\prime}}(\mathbb{T}^{3}))
for any
\beta^{\prime}<\beta
. Moreover, using the Euler equations, that
u\in C^{\beta^{\prime\prime}}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{3})
for any
\beta^{\prime\prime}<\beta^{\prime}
. Since
\beta^{\prime\prime}<\beta^{\prime}<\beta<1/3
is arbitrary in this argument, by renaming
\beta^{\prime\prime}
to
\beta
we deduce the existence of
u\in C^{\beta}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{3})
as in the statement of Theorem
3.1
. In particular, using (
2.6
) and taking the limit implies that
e(t)=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\left|u\right|^{2}\,dx
. The inequality (
3.1
) follows from (
2.10
) and (
2.66
), whereas the inequality (
3.2
) follows from (
2.67
), (
2.17
) and (
2.21
).

Step 3. Choice of inertial scale.
Having constructed the vector field
u
with active length-scales
\lambda^{-1}_{q}
,
q\geq q_{0}
, our next goal is to choose the length scale (more precisely, the index
q_{I}
), below which small-scale advection becomes dominant. To this end let
\rho_{in}\in\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{3})\setminus\{0\}
and define its characteristic length-scale as

\ell_{in}:=\frac{\|\rho_{in}\|_{L^{2}}}{\|\nabla\rho_{in}\|_{L^{2}}}.

We fix
q_{I}\geq q_{0}
so that

10C_{\tilde{N}}\ell_{in}^{-1}\leq\lambda_{q}\left(1-e^{-2\kappa_{q+1}\ell_{0}^{-2}T}\right)^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\quad\textrm{ for all }q\geq q_{I},

(3.10)

where
C_{\tilde{N}}\geq 1
is a constant (depending only on
\tilde{N}
from Step 1) related to mollification as follows:
We fix a standard symmetric mollifier
\psi
(one can also use e.g.
\psi
from Section
7.1
) and set
\psi_{r}(x)=r^{-3}\psi(\frac{x}{r})
. Then we have (cf. Lemma
7.2
) for all
g\in\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{3})

\displaystyle\|g*\psi_{r}-g\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\tilde{N}}r\|\nabla g\|_{L^{2}},

(3.11)

\displaystyle\|g*\psi_{r}\|_{H^{n+1}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\tilde{N}}r^{-n}\|\nabla g\|_{L^{2}}\textrm{ for all }n\leq\tilde{N}.

To see that the choice of
q_{I}
is possible, we verify that the expression on the right hand side of (
3.10
) is monotone increasing in
q
. To see this, in view of (
2.27
) it suffices to show that the function

f(\lambda):=\lambda^{2}(1-e^{-2\lambda^{-b\theta}})

(3.12)

is strictly monotone increasing for
\lambda\in(0,\infty)
with
f(\lambda)\to\infty
as
\lambda\to\infty
. First of all, the asymptotic behaviour as
\lambda\to\infty
is easily deduced from the Taylor expansion of the exponential, which gives
f(\lambda)=O(\lambda^{2-b\theta})
. Recalling the choice of
\theta
from (
2.27
) as well as
b<\sqrt{\tfrac{3}{2}}
and
\beta<\frac{1}{3}
, we obtain
b\theta<2
, showing that
\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}f(\lambda)=\infty
. Then, computing the derivative we obtain

f^{\prime}(\lambda)=2\lambda\left(1-(1+\tfrac{1}{2}b\theta\lambda^{-b\theta})e^{-\lambda^{-b\theta}}\right)=2\lambda\left(1-g(\lambda^{-b\theta})\right),

where
g(s)=(1+\tfrac{1}{2}b\theta s)e^{-s}
. Observe that
g(0)=1
and
g(s)\to 0
as
s\to\infty
. On the one hand direct computation shows that
g^{\prime}(s)=0
only if
s=1-\frac{2}{b\theta}
. Using again that
b\theta<2
we deduce that
g
has no local maxima/minima in
(0,\infty)
. Consequently
g(s)<1
for all
s>0
, implying that
f
is strictly monotone increasing on
(0,\infty)
.

Our goal in the remaining steps is to show that the solution
\rho_{\kappa}
of (
1.1
) can be approximated by the solution
\tilde{\rho}_{q_{I}}
of the equation
\partial_{t}\tilde{\rho}_{q_{I}}+u_{q_{I}}\cdot\nabla\tilde{\rho}_{q_{I}}=\kappa_{q_{I}}\Delta\tilde{\rho}_{q_{I}}
.

Step 4. Mollification of initial data.
Define

\tilde{\rho}_{in}:=\rho_{in}*\psi_{r}\quad\text{ with }\quad r=\lambda^{-1}_{q_{I}}

(3.13)

where
\psi
is the mollifier of Step 3. Inequalities (
3.11
) and the definition of
\ell_{in}
imply

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\rho}_{in}-\rho_{in}\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\tilde{N}}\lambda_{q_{I}}^{-1}\ell_{in}^{-1}\|\rho_{in}\|_{L^{2}}

(3.14)

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\rho}_{in}\|_{H^{n+1}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\tilde{N}}\lambda_{q_{I}}^{n}\ell_{in}^{-1}\|\rho_{in}\|_{L^{2}}\quad\textrm{ for all }0\leq n\leq\tilde{N}.

(3.15)

From (
3.14
) together (
3.10
) we deduce

\|\tilde{\rho}_{in}-\rho_{in}\|_{L^{2}}\leq\tfrac{1}{10}\|\rho_{in}\|_{L^{2}}\,.

(3.16)

Step 5. Enhanced dissipation in the inertial regime.
For
q\geq q_{I}
, consider the solution
\tilde{\rho}_{q}
of the equation

\begin{split}\partial_{t}\tilde{\rho}_{q}+u_{q}\cdot\nabla\tilde{\rho}_{q}=&\kappa_{q}\Delta\tilde{\rho}_{q}\,,\\
\tilde{\rho}_{q}|_{t=0}=&\tilde{\rho}_{in}\,.\end{split}

(3.17)

Using the Poincaré inequality in conjunction with the classical energy estimate on (
3.17
) and Gronwall’s inequality, we obtain the following lower bound on the dissipation rate:

\begin{split}\tilde{D}_{q}&:=\kappa_{q}\int_{0}^{T}\|\nabla\tilde{\rho}_{q}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}dt\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\left(\|\tilde{\rho}_{in}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}-\|\tilde{\rho}_{q}(T)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right)\\
&\geq\frac{1}{2}\left(1-\exp(-2\ell^{-2}_{0}\kappa_{q}T)\right)\|\tilde{\rho}_{in}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.\end{split}

(3.18)

Using (
3.10
) and the fact that
\kappa_{q+1}\leq\kappa_{q}
we then estimate from below as

\begin{split}\tilde{D}_{q}&\geq 50C_{\tilde{N}}^{2}\ell_{in}^{-2}\lambda_{q}^{-2}\|\tilde{\rho}_{in}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\\
&\overset{\textrm{\eqref{e:comparerhotilderho}}}{\geq}40C_{\tilde{N}}^{2}\ell_{in}^{-2}\lambda_{q}^{-2}\|\rho_{in}\|_{L^{2}}^{2},\end{split}

and similarly we obtain the same lower bound for
\tilde{D}_{q+1}
. In turn, applying (
3.15
), we deduce

\|\tilde{\rho}_{in}\|_{H^{n+1}}\leq\tfrac{1}{6}\lambda_{q_{I}}^{n+1}\min\{\tilde{D}_{q}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}},\tilde{D}_{q+1}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\}\quad\textrm{ for any }0\leq n\leq\tilde{N}.

Therefore Proposition
2.4
is applicable to the equation (
3.17
) for any
q\geq q_{I}
. We obtain
